"Here they are, sir, at last."
The threshing of the screw ceased forthwith and a

A powerful figure strode towards them.
"So you managed it, eh? You're lucky, and-" The

Why was it, he reflected, that he derived such pleasure
from baiting Rufus Reilly? The man, once he lost his
temper and ran berserk, could be a very ugly customer,
and Higgins, besides being unarmed, was slightly below
par as a result of his recent activities. Anyhow, he was
wasting time on purely private pleasures.
"You may be right about Stanmore," he said placat-
ingly, "but it's up to me to make sure, with or without
your permission. I want neither help nor hindrance from
you, Reilly-see that I don't get it." Inspector Higgins
turned to the others. "I'm sick o' searching this ship,
but let's get at it."
Inspector Higgins once more conjured up the vision
of his own successful concealment on this very ship, which,
together with the thought that scarcely a week passed
without a stowaway being reported by some captain or
other after hiding aboard for days, and the fact that this
ship had been thoroughly searched already, did not help
matters. Of course, the previous search had been unsuc-
cessful because Foxy-face and Co. were already ashore,
but Foxy-face himself was known to be somewhere in the
offing, and it was exactly four times as easy for one man
to hide than for four.
And a ship seemed to be comprised entirely of hiding-
places. Unlike a house, there were innumerable cupboards
and cubbyholes, each capable of concealing a man.
Also there was a great deal of truth in Reilly's naïve
admission that he would never have welcomed the police
aboard had Foxy-face already been hidden. And every-
thing seemed the same as on the previous search... save
that the anchor was now up and then down.252
INSPECTOR HIGGINS HURRIES
Higgins scratched his head speculatively at the thought.
He remembered his own idea that the flukes of the anchor
might make a most effective hiding-place. H'm. Yet
the anchor had only just been raised. Still, there had been
plenty of time for Stanmore to clamber over the bows.
Well, well, well!
Higgins grinned to himself and beckoned with crooked

wave."
With an air of profound mystery he led the officer

As though in answer to his question, there was the
merest suggestion of a breeze and the mist began to roll

Attached to the anchor by a steel hawser were three
tin boxes !
No wonder the anchor had been weighed so slowly and
with such apparent care! No wonder their previous search
(whilst the ship had been riding at anchor) had been so
unsuccessful.
Higgins grinned delightedly. This might prove better
than Foxy-face. He turned to Rufus Reilly and surprised
a look of malignant hate on the man's face.
"Do you think a couple of your crew could give me

"I've changed me mind."5.30 P.M.
253
Reilly snapped his fingers and beckoned to two

they were safely on deck.
Inspector Higgins stared at the boxes with a puzzled

Daydream had been ordered inshore by the River Police

captain must have raised the anchor. Why hadn't the
boxes then been seen? Surely they couldn't have been
attached to the anchor subsequently?
Then his brow cleared. Got it! The river police had
reported that the Daydream had dragged anchor, so it
had never actually been hauled above water.

An iron bar crashed to the deck.
CHAPTER XXXIII
6.10 P.M.
INSPECTOR HIGGINS was round in a flash. One glance
was sufficient to take in this swift turn of events. The
sudden sight of such vast wealth had tipped the scales
-Reilly and his cut-throat crew were now out of hand.
Quick advantage had been taken of the careless absorption
of the two police officers in the contents of the tin box,
and it was only the other officer's cry of alarm which had
saved the inspector's life. Where the remaining couple
of officers were and what had happened to them Higgins
did not know, but he could guess. And to think that
he himself had handed Rufus Reilly the iron bar which
had nearly ended his own existence. Lord, what a
fool he had been to have opened the boxes on board this
ship! Why the deuce hadn't he curbed his curiosity?
He dodged aside and avoided a vicious sweep from
Reilly.
The River Police officer was already struggling with
a couple of seamen, and . .
•6.10 P.M.
257
Another blind rush from Reilly which Higgins side-
stepped. He lunged and caught the man a glancing
blow, and nearly overbalanced in the process. The deck
was slippery as a result of the recent fog, and the heavy
sea-boots were not conducive to fancy footwork.
Where the blazes was his police-whistle? At the
bottom of the rowing-boat alongside, of course! Hell!
Higgins smashed out with his left and had the huge
satisfaction of connecting with Reilly's ear.
The man
staggered backwards, tripped over the tin box, scattering
jewels right and left, and quickly jumped up and
scampered out of reach. Unfortunately he still retained
his grip of the iron bar.
The inspector had a brief respite. A confused heap
a couple of yards away indicated where the other officer
was getting the worst of his argument with the two
seamen. Higgins jumped to his aid. He struck with all
the force at his command, yet with a faint regret that he
was attacking from behind, and the topmost man slithered
a few feet along the deck and lay supine on his face.
There was no time for more, for Reilly, grasping the
iron bar in his enormous hand and with a long-bladed
knife in the other, was advancing at a crouch. Higgins
wondered why the man did not use the gun which he
knew him to possess, and immediately guessed the
reason.
The sound of a shot would attract attention from the
shore, and Reilly couldn't afford this. He could not
escape with the priceless booty unless he sneaked away.
Once the police aboard were overpowered he could
steam a little way down the river and then abandon ship
with the loot.
That being the case:
"Hi-yi-hi-yi !"'
R258
INSPECTOR HIGGINS HURRIES
A tremendous yell from the inspector, which he
instantly regretted, for, with a snarl, Rufus Reilly hurled

pocket.
The iron bar clattered to the deck and Higgins
immediately pounced upon it. Now he'd show
'em!
He charged at Reilly and lunged at his gun hand. A
scream of pain and Reilly's arm hung limply at his side.
He turned and fled aft. Brandishing the bar, the inspector
dashed in pursuit. Reilly was making for the bridge.
Why?
Perhaps there was another gun in the chartroom—or,
more likely, he wished to signal to the engine-room
below to get the vessel on the move, and thus re-
move the likelihood of reinforcements from the
shore. That would be dangerous without someone at
the wheel.
Footsteps behind him. Higgins turned. It was the
River Police officer, a livid scratch down one cheek, one

"Kill 'em all, Higgy boy!" he roared. "Me blood's
up!"
Higgins blinked at the man's ferocity.
"Get below and help the others!" he shouted. "I'll

Higgins clambered up the companionway, then crept

what Higgins the landsman called a veranda, and
Higgins was acutely conscious that Reilly might be

259
Or he might have made a complete circuit and even

Higgins knew he must take some sort of chance. He

Higgins was about to rush blindly up the iron ladder

weighed anchor? And no real seaman would leave such
a post whatever the excitements on deck. Then Reilly
was not alone on the bridge. Of course, with such a crew
discipline might be very slack, yet . . .
Higgins leaned over the rail and gazed aft—then

INSPECTOR HIGGINS HURRIES

Higgins gazed round the deserted bridge. How the

vague idea that to steer a ship to the right one turned
the wheel to the left, or something like that! A bit too
confused for his way of thinking. But to stop it?
Ah, yes. A sort of clock face with speeds ahead and

261
H'm. Of course the thing wasn't exactly automatic, it

of the crew had not hurried to their captain's assistance,
although it was quite likely, the speed things had
happened, that they were as yet unacquainted with the
trend of affairs on deck-unless the River Police officer
had enlightened them. There must be an explanation
somewhere, but . . .
And the explanation was not far to seek.
Standing over a hatchway, and dancing about in utter
disgust, was the officer in question.
"They can't get out, Higgy, and I can't get in. Ain't
it the last note ?"
"What about him?" queried Higgins, indicating a man
lying insensible alongside the hatchway.
"Him? Reilly must have sent him to lock 'em in.
I did that!"—proudly, nodding towards the recumbent
figure.
A ray of enlightenment. Reilly must have acted
remarkably quickly when he had first spotted the jewels,
or this man must have been extraordinarily quick-witted.262
INSPECTOR HIGGINS HURRIES
It was ironical that Reilly, through greediness, had caused
his own downfall. For it was fairly obvious that this man
had been sent (or had acted on his own initiative) to pen
the stokers and engine-room staff below, in order that
there should be a bigger share-out of the jewels above,
and had thus prevented any rescue. Very, very satis-
fying.
"Hi, you!" bellowed Higgins to the men imprisoned
below. "You'd better stop the engines unless you want
to go to Kingdom Come !"
A voice indicated that the inspector could go in the
other direction! However, someone below showed more
sense of their danger, for with a sigh the engines ceased
their throbbing.
"Where's the other two?" queried Higgins. "These
are safer where they are."
"Blowed if I know. Gosh Almighty, sir, but here's
some more."
Inspector Higgins turned quickly, to see a burly

was sufficient-he beamed a welcome. It was Chief-

"Hallo, Higgy. Having a spot o' bother?"
"I think it's been amicably settled, sir"-diffidently.
"How did you come aboard ?”
"Walked"-sarcastically. His relief at finding the

The squad at Dryan's command soon had matters

warlike officer in charge, one of whom was still lying

Inspector Higgins leaned over the side. Seated on the

It was Foxy-face Stanmore.

They watched the motor-boat lowered from the

As a matter of actual fact, the inspector knew exactly

"Why?"
"To chuck in the engines below. If I'm to get the sack

Higgins grinned. "I'll speak to the boss," he

269
three boats had been got in readiness, and he walked

And then all eyes were turned shoreward, for from

The local policemen stood like hounds at the leash,
and a vague unrest seemed to permeate the batch of
prisoners. With the former, there appeared to be an
air of expectancy over and above that general tenseness
which affects policemen on hearing the recognized call
for aid. Inspector Higgins himself, though not imbued
with any local spirit, could yet feel . . .
And then it came.
A long drawn-out wail from a siren in the middle of the
town, like that of a factory hooter. Immediately there was
a stampede for the boats. For one brief instant Higgins
stared in amazement, then at last he found his voice.
"Hi! Hi! What's the big idea?"
The rush was stemmed, and the local police stared at
him in wonderment.
"General call, sir," volunteered one of the men.
"Must be a riot or something in town."
"What of it? Your job's here. Six of you stay behind
to look after the gang here. Chief Dryan, will you take
charge here?"270
INSPECTOR HIGGINS HURRIES
"I suppose so. I wouldn't be much use ashore."
"Thank you, sir. Now the rest of us will push off-and

Higgins ran to the ship's side and called to the first

"What about you, sir? Can you send a landing-party

The naval officer was horrified at the suggestion.
"Daren't," he stated laconically. "Simply asking
for a court martial. Sorry! Red tape an' all that !''
"I see. Come on, the rest of you."
Willing, if inexpert hands, seized hold of the oars
when all were aboard the three rowing-boats, whilst
their amateur efforts were watched with sneering contempt
by the batch of maritime prisoners, and with unconcealed
envy by half a dozen disconsolate colleagues remaining
behind. The river policemen were too busy with their
damaged launch to take any interest whatever!
Half-way ashore the repetition of the blast on the siren
at Horton spurred the rowers to renewed efforts.
"They waited till a batch of us were on special duty,"
gasped one constable. "It's always the same. They don't
start trouble till there's no one to stop it."
"Can't blame 'em for that," murmured a more
philosophical colleague.
"Wonder what they're up to now."
"We'll soon know.”
As the boats grounded the police were assailed with a
hail of stones from otherwise disinterested longshoremen,
who vanished as soon as the landing was accomplished.
Despite their impatience to be up and doing, Higgins
gave a few words of warning.
"Surprise, if possible, will be the key to success.7 P.M.
271
Secondly, we must keep together. And it's no use galloping
like a mad horse. The first to arrive, if alone, will have
no interest in the end of the scrap. That's all. Kim
on !"
A gentle jog-trot towards the kernel of the disturbance,
an occasional blast on a police-whistle giving guidance,
and, spurred on by the jeering maledictions of the feminine
populace, the police squad hurried on its way.
"Gosh, sir, but it's mighty close to the station!"
Inspector Higgins was amazed at the general aspect
of squalor of the streets, now that the fog had lifted, and
was mighty glad that his general duties kept him well
clear of the district.
"Ber-limey! It is the station."
Even from the distance Higgins could make out the
shattered skeleton of the blue lamp which helped to
indicate Horton police station. Around the doors milled
a mass of mankind, as yet unaware of the approaching
reinforcements.
"Look aht! Look aht!" screamed a blowzy virago.
"'Ere's the slops !"
Her voice could be heard plainly above the sound
of the tumult, and a few hangers-on at the fringe of the
affray gave one quick glance at the oncoming horde and
then slunk discreetly away.
At this precise moment Inspector Higgins made the
startling discovery that he had no weapon, which in the
excitement he had hitherto overlooked. All his com-
panions were armed with serviceable truncheons, of which
there is no more effective weapon in a riot. Too late now,
anyhow.
"Let 'em have it, boys!"
Their initial charge cut a path almost to the door
of the station, but there they met some unexpected272
INSPECTOR HIGGINS HURRIES
opposition from a gang of toughs armed with weighted
stockings-the weights consisting (as Higgins learned
when one stocking burst all over him!) of metal nuts,
door-knobs and similar junk. But soon the pressure was
so great that such weapons were at a discount, there
being insufficient room to manipulate them, and thus
Inspector Higgins came into his own.
With a series of vicious short-arm jabs he forged a
way through the main door of the police station, leaving
a trail of sorry youths in his wake. Once within the
doors, matters were easier. There was not the congestion
for one thing, and, further, those who had been fighting
to get in were now scrambling to get out. He tried to
step aside, but then felt himself being carried out again
with the stream, and nothing he could do would stem the
flood. For one terrible instant his arms were pinned to
his side by sheer force of numbers, and in that enforced
period of inaction he saw passing out of the doors of the
station Hefty and Baker.
Jerusalem! The reason for this attack on the station
was immediately apparent. And to all intents and
purposes it had succeeded, unless he himself could do
something. He let out a roar :
"Stick it, boys! Let 'em have it !"
An easement in the crush and his arms were freed.
"Shut yer mouth," snarled a vicious voice close at hand.
Higgins lashed out-the owner of the voice vanished
beneath the surging mob. Slowly the inspector forged
a way towards Hefty and Baker, avoiding blows as best
he might, yet with the awful thought that he was being
slowly overwhelmed by sheer weight of numbers.
A lightning glance around showed no friendly face.
He was cut off from the rest of his squad-or they had
already been placed hors de combat.9.30 P.M.
273

And then slowly it dawned on his consciousness
that the police were fighting a losing battle.
realization caused a slackening of his efforts. A burly

he laid hold of the couple nearest to him and they all fell
to the ground together. A milling mob which suddenly

He blinked. Extraordinary!
And then a crowd of about fifty bluejackets charged
upon the scene, with the burly first lieutenant well in
the van.
CHAPTER XXXV
9.30 P.M.
INSPECTOR HIGGINS staggered to his feet and pointed
to the crowd still surging round Hefty and Baker. He
could not raise another gallop-having had enough
running for one day.
"Grab 'em all !" he roared.
The crowd turned to fight, but, in the face of that
advancing horde of sailors, changed its mind and fled,
whilst the bluejackets cheered and charged in pursuit.
Inspector Higgins passed a weary hand over his brow
and slowly crossed the threshold of the police station.
The place was a shambles. The charge-room, so neat and
tidy ordinarily, was in a state of the utmost confusion.
Unconscious across his desk was the unthinking sergeant
S274
INSPECTOR HIGGINS HURRIES
who had asked Higgins what charge should be recorded
against the names of Hefty and Baker in the charge-

"It must have broke !" he said.

"This chain. You see, when I pull the chain it blows

The chain fell from his fingers and he slipped to the

But the wires of the telephone instrument had been
wrenched from the wall. On the superintendent's desk

had no record of the calls emanating from the box
near the "Mermaid". And the corridor to the cells
was carpeted with casualties. Higgins daintily picked
his way to the cell which had housed Hefty and Baker.
It had been opened with a key, yet no attempt had been
made to open the other cells, through the judases of which
the unfortunate inmates stared with fear-stricken eyes.
It was obvious that the gaoler had been forced somehow
to release his charges.
Although Inspector Higgins had been convinced ever
since he first caught sight of the released Hefty and
Baker that they supplied the sole reason for the attack
on the police station, yet the fact that none other had been9.30 P.M.
275
freed proved beyond doubt that the whole episode had
been engineered for their benefit.
And yet a few hours back Horton had probably been
unaware of their very existence! This mysterious chief
must have a colossal pull in the underworld. He hoped
his opportune if unexpected nautical reinforcements
managed to rope him in. A dangerous man that, and
not likely to give in without a struggle-and armed, with-
out a doubt. He hoped none of the sailors would be hurt
that way.
The inspector could not make out why that effective
means of dispersing a hostile crowd, the hosepipe; had not
been brought into play, but guessed that the attack had
been too swift for such a defence.
The attack, too, considering the speed with which it
must have been planned, could not have taken cognizance
of the departure of a squad to help Higgins on the
Daydream. That was merely a lucky break for the storm-
ing party. The gang chief must have been prepared to
attack a full force of police at the station, and to find on
arrival but a skeleton staff was indeed good fortune-for
him!
Yet why was it essential for him to rescue Hefty
and Baker at all? They had already double-crossed him
once according to their inadvertent confession, although
Hefty's half-hearted "blowing of the gaff" might have
had something to do with it. There were two possible
reasons, either of which (or a combination of the two)
might have been the real reason. The gang chief might
have feared that either of them would, to save his own
skin, reveal the chief's identity, or his own power in
the underworld might have depended largely upon the
protection he afforded his underlings when in peril, and
the attack on the station was in the nature of a beau geste.276
INSPECTOR HIGGINS HURRIES
And, to all intents and purposes, the attack had
succeeded.
A trampling of many feet outside the station. Was

He didn't think so, now that Hefty and Baker had

Baker in the cooler at Horton, Robinson at Sungate,
Stanmore at the Horton sub-station, Lofty Younger at
the Yard, and Charlie Younger in the mortuary. There's
the 'six of us' according to Hefty when we were at
Sungate! Very, very satisfying."
"And the king-pin of all goes free."
"Oh, we'll get him right enough. Sure you don't
mind me leaving you here? Right! Night-night!"'
Inspector Higgins watched the hired car slip away in
the darkness, and then began homeward to plod his
weary way, painfully self-conscious at his peculiar attire.
Sea-boots at Wembley were incongruous enough, but a
pair of woolly overalls and borrowed sweater and jacket !
Higgins shuddered.
Fortunately for his own peace of mind he didn't meet
a soul until he turned into his own road, and there, in
the distance, he recognized the slim form of his wife,
leading-leading a Shetland pony? No! Cussed if it
wasn't that dog!
"Hallo, Higgy dear !" Then she tittered: "You do
look funny!" Higgins scowled whilst he tried to escape
the dog's eager embrace. "Poor Bruce! I'm taking him
out for a run. He's been very, very badly treated, Higgy.
Cuts and scars all over. The brutes! And he's such a9.30 P.M.
279
friendly soul, Higgy. I'm sure you'll enjoy taking him
for runs at nights."
"I'll-what?" said Inspector Higgins, scandalized,
as a long vista of endless evening walks stretched before
him. "What he wants is a good kick in the slats to teach
him manners and . . ."
"He's had a good many already, Higgy"-quietly.
Higgins grinned. "Sorry, Bruce." He scratched the
animal's head, and it licked his hand for more. Then of
a sudden it bristled, growled and bared its teeth.
Walking towards them with stereotyped measured
tread was a uniformed constable. Higgins visualized
a series of troubles if the brute was afflicted with an
aversion to anyone in uniform--a form of antipathy from
which many dogs suffer. He took a turn in the lead
attached to the dog's new collar and braced his feet.
"Good evening, sir.”
It was Constable Thomson.
"Good evening."
"Back on the old beat, sir. I wonder if you'll support
my application for extra pay for my journey to Sungate
last night, sir?"

"Thank you, sir. Good night. Good night, madam."
With a gracious patting of Bruce's snarling head and a

Thomson? Thomson? Yet when Constable Thomson
had visited that house at Sungate Bruce here had been

but had made no noise. Why not, if the dog was so

to that-Constable Thomson had not caused the dog's

Gripping the dog's collar, Inspector Higgins slipped
the lead and released him.
As soon as he had done so Inspector Higgins realized
he had made a mistake, for Bruce streaked off down the
street and was lost in the darkness almost before Higgins
was aware he was off.
Higgins turned to his wife.
"Run after that slop and bring him back, will you,
please, dear? I've-er-just thought of something."
Muriel stood indecisively for one moment, then
abruptly turned on her heel and set off after Constable
Thomson.
Then Inspector Higgins, after an apprehensive glance
in the wake of the vanished Bruce, furtively made his
way towards his home, keeping well in the shadows.
Perhaps it wouldn't be at all a bad idea to telephone for
assistance if what he anticipated should turn out to be
correct. Had his telephone been put in order? In any
case his own instrument should be the last he should
use! Better get his neighbour Brewster to come to the
rescue again. That wasn't a bad idea. Inspector Higgins
slunk through an open gate.
He gave a gentle tap on the outer door-then waited.
He turned and gave a glance at his own house opposite.
It suddenly struck him that were Brewster to turn on
the hall light (as he without doubt would do), then
Higgins himself would stand out in bold relief to any
watcher near at hand. Gosh! What a fool he had been
to knock at all. He hoped that when Brewster opened
the door he wouldn't get a bullet for his pains. Better
make for the back entrance and rouse Brewster from there.9.30 P.M.
281
With infinite care Higgins crept round to the back of
the house. And the back door was on the jar.
"Hsst! Brewster! Brewster !"-a stage whisper.
And then an awful thought. Perhaps Brewster had
seen something last night which he shouldn't have done-
and had been effectively silenced before he could give
anything away. Had that been the reason for the chief's
journey to Wembley in Eaton's taxicab? Higgins struck
a match. There upon the doormat a bunch of keys-
four keys, one "obviously" new and home-made. Higgins
recognized them at once. Found on Younger's body and
taken from Higgins by the chief himself.
A gust of air extinguished the match.
Inspector Higgins felt his hair rise on end.
The mysterious chief had been here!
With infinite care Higgins closed the back door and
began to feel his way through the house, fearful at what
he might stumble upon at any moment, his ears alert
for the slightest sound, his eyes useless in the pitch dark-
ness.
He took his courage in his hands and struck another
match-and was well rewarded. In an alcove against the
front door was an electric torch.
Shielding the ray as best he might with his hand,
the inspector carefully illuminated the stairs, then slowly
mounted them. The mysterious chief might or might not
be present-but prevention was better than cure! He
was hardly likely to have waited after . .
Higgins flung open a door-then ducked. Nothing
happened.
Slowly it was borne upon him that the house was
untenanted—at least, by any living thing.282
INSPECTOR HIGGINS HURRIES
CHAPTER XXXVI
10.30 P.M.
INSPECTOR HIGGINS, having arrived at this conclusion,
brazenly descended the stairs to the hall and telephoned
to the Wembley police station for a squad to comb the
district for the chief. He had a shrewd idea as to where the
chief was hiding and why he was so hiding-but he might
be wrong.
Once more upstairs for a quick look round. The front
room. A quick flash with the torch should reveal all
there was likely to be.
Surprisingly, it revealed a gun.
Higgins lifted the weapon and carried it to the back
of the house where he could examine it without revealing
his presence to anyone in the street. Boldly he turned on
the light.
A gun right enough—but a weird sort of affair. And
And the bore
what was Brewster doing with a gun?
was ribbed. Higgins had never seen the like before, and
Jerusalem! Those daggers!
·
A flood of enlightenment. Those daggers were
actually fired from this gun! No wonder he had thought
the "thrower" to be a powerful man! No wonder there
was such precision of aim!
Those bizarre handles to the daggers, a cross-section
of which he had likened to a cog-wheel of a watch, were
made to fit the rifling of this gun !
Then Brewster was the chief!
No wonder he had felled that tree in his front garden.
Higgins essayed a gentle peep from the front room
in which he had found the gun, and immediately saw that10.30 P.M.
283
the felled tree left a clear line of sight from the window
to his own front door!
Such foresight! Charles Younger had been killed
with this very weapon. Yet why had he been killed?
Either in mistake for Higgins himself, or Brewster had
recognized him, realized that his appearance on Higgins's
doorstep was tantamount to a confession of a double-
cross, and had slain him for the traitor he was.
Then what had happened? Higgins had come along
with the secretary of the Society of Writers and had
discovered the body. Then Constable Thomson had
arrived on the scene, and Higgins had sent him over here
to 'phone-and he had 'phoned from here!
Brewster must have awaited his opportunity to search
Higgins's house for the Waiters file. He had fired another
dagger at Higgins with the object of getting him out of
the way. He had missed, and had then hit on the idea
of luring away the police by the sound of running footsteps.
Constable Thomson had chased and lost him whilst
Higgins himself was chasing the others to the car.
Brewster had been unable to make any search until
much later in the night, but he had made it. What
was it either Baker or Hefty had said at Horton police
station? "He tries to make too sure-that's what's
his trouble."
And then on a sideboard the inspector caught the
reflection, on a piece of glass, of the street lamp. It dis-
tracted his attention, and he felt his thoughts wandering.
He crossed the room-then he smiled a crooked smile
of satisfaction. The piece of glass was a photographic
plate, and alongside the plate was a print. There was
the local street of purely local interest, there was the
well-dressed benevolent old gentleman from whom
Walters was supposed to have stolen the watch-that284
INSPECTOR HIGGINS HURRIES
gentleman whom the police now knew to be exactly
what he seemed-and there, in the middle distance,
wonderfully clear-cut, was Mr. Brewster himself.
Why hadn't he seen the likeness before? Of course, this
plate and photograph had been hidden in the file and
Higgins hadn't given any thought to the other photos
in the scene. And Brewster was a comparative newcomer
to the district.
Inspector Higgins made many mental excuses for
himself-but was not satisfied.
And where was Brewster now? Higgins grinned
sardonically. Brewster made too sure, did he? Then
why hadn't he shot Higgins a few moments back when he
had been walking towards his house? Obviously he had
thought the inspector to be a floating corpse near
Horton.
Yet if he had taken any personal part in the abortive
attempt to rescue Hefty and Baker from Horton police
station he must have seen Inspector Higgins when he
arrived on the scene. Then why hadn't he done anything
in the matter, if he made so sure? Had he lost his nerve?
From his recollection of the man in that overhanging
room at Horton Higgins could not accept the suggestion.
Then what?
A thought-and Inspector Higgins nodded grimly.
That was it! His bizarre costume had proved an effective
disguise! Brewster, waiting for a certainty, had been
fooled-unless he was waiting somewhere where he could
not be fooled-unless he was waiting in the inspector's
own house! And-and-at any moment Muriel and
Constable Thomson might stumble upon him.
Higgins clattered down the stairs to the back door,
gave a quick sigh of relief as his hasty glance down the
street proved that Muriel had not yet returned, then,10.30 P.M.
285
*
avoiding the street lamp, crossed the road and crept
furtively up to his gate. This was a chance he was
compelled to take. He vaulted the gate and was beneath
the shelter of the porch in a second. There he stood and
mopped his brow, for he had miscalculated the weight
of the sea-boots and had almost stumbled when vaulting.
And then his heart gave a bound, for someone was
creeping from the back of the house-he could hear the
footsteps. Then he blew out his cheeks in thankfulness
as the footsteps "materialized" into Bruce the mastiff.
The dog, usually so affectionate, completely ignored him
and started sniffing at the crack between the front door
and the jamb.
A whimper of excitement, a growl of menace, then
Bruce like a caged tiger took half-turns left and right in
front of the closed door.
"You owe him something, don't you, Bruce boy?"
muttered Higgins. "An' so do I. I've got a wife to think
of, but your many offspring don't even know you, an'
won't miss you. I'll let you in and you can take your
chance. Oh, blast!"
Higgins realized he had no door-key.
"Come on, boy.'
Round to the back. If Muriel had followed her usual
practice the back door would not be locked, or, if locked,
he knew where to find the key-and if he couldn't, then
he would break in . . . it would be an experience to

It was not locked! With infinite care he turned the
handle then his attempt at silence was set at naught,
for with a snarl of fury Bruce squirmed past him and
dashed into the house.
Inspector Higgins had already decided that the
mysterious chief would be at the front of the house,286
INSPECTOR HIGGINS HURRIES
so that he could watch the street. He might have seen
the inspector entering his own house, but, not recognizing
him in his bizarre outfit, might have assumed him to be
a humble back-door sneak-thief. Yet why hadn't he
seen Higgins when he had crossed the road and vaulted

cupboard? Or . . .
The snarling Bruce dashed straight up the stairs-
thus removing any need for further speculation.
A cry of alarm from upstairs and the hasty slamming
of a door.
Brewster was there right enough! What a nerve the
man had to wait with murder in his heart for Higgins
to return home! That he had a certain nerve was proved
by his audacious attack on Chief-Inspector Dryan at
Scotland Yard. But he must now be past caring what the
outcome might be, and thus was the more dangerous !
Quietly the inspector mounted the stairs, confident
that Bruce could keep the man engaged until he himself
could attack with a reasonable chance of success.
What weapon would the man have? Not a gun, or
he would surely have used it by now! Then a knife!
He seemed to have a preference for knives-that attack
on the taxi-driver, Eaton. Eaton ought to be able to
identify him when the time came—if it came !
A snarl which changed to a whelp of pain. The back
room.
Higgins flung open the door.
Penned into the further corner, held at bay by a
growling Bruce, was the dim figure of a man. Inspector
Higgins deflected the light switch. Nothing happened.
So the wires had been tampered with. Quick worker, this
Brewster. Must have waited till Muriel went out with
the dog..10.30 P.M.
287
A savage kick at the dog which hurtled it against the
wall, and the man charged at Higgins, who, weaponless,
skipped aside, and in skipping tripped over a footstool.
Quick to seize the advantage, Brewster leapt at his fallen
adversary with upraised knife, but the dog, with bared
fangs, jumped to meet him. Brewster dashed from
the room with the mastiff at his heels.
Inspector Higgins jumped to his feet and followed.
Barred from the stairs by the dog, Brewster ran into
the front room and tried to slam the door, but Bruce's
sturdy form intervened.
Again Higgins followed, and again almost automatically
he deflected the light switch. This time the room was
flooded with light. Brewster must only have removed
the bulb from the other room. At bay against the window
was Brewster, a look of bestial hate upon his features,
a dripping knife in his hand. A yard away, half-cringing,
half-defiant, crouched as though to spring, with a slashed
shoulder, was the mastiff.
Inspector Higgins turned and locked the door, hurling
the key past Brewster's head through the window. That
was another window gone, but this one he would have to
pay for! And the tinkling smash should warn Muriel
and Constable Thomson that there was trouble
brewing.
"Now, Brewster, I've got you where I want you !"
"You think so, eh?" The man was obviously past
caring what happened to him. The snarling dog had
sapped his morale. He hurled the knife at Higgins, and
it struck the wall more than a yard away; then he turned
to the window. The moment he took his eyes from the
dog's the animal jumped at his throat. Such was the
impetus that there was a smashing of glass and a splinter-
ing of wood. The whole framework of the window bulged288
INSPECTOR HIGGINS HURRIES
outwards. Higgins stood appalled, then crossed the room
with swift strides. After all, the man was human!
Brewster toppled backwards and slowly overbalanced.
Bruce snapped at an ankle with vicious teeth; Higgins
clutched at the other foot and missed. An awful, ear-
splitting, terrifying scream cut short in the middle by a
nauseating crash. Then silence.
Half fearfully the inspector peered over the broken
window-frame.
On his front doorstep was another dead body.
At last all was peace. Inspector Higgins thought he
might as well glance through his morning paper, as yet
unopened, before retiring to his well-earned rest. And
there, on the front page, was a photograph of himself.
Eagerly he scanned the newsprint :
SOCIETY OF WRITERS' DINNER
INSPECTOR HIGGINS SPEAKS.
Speaking at the coming-of-age dinner of the Society of
Writers last evening, Inspector Higgins shattered some well-
fostered illusions of the general public . . . sheer monotony of
his existence. .
very dull.
Higgins glanced at the clock on the mantelpiece.
Twenty-four hours ago, almost to the minute, he had been
making this speech. He sighed.
Jerusalem! What a day!
